ENGLISHWOMAN IN PAPUA 

Miss Evelyn Cheesman, formerly 
curator of Insects at. the: London 
Zoo, has returned from an eigh- 
teen, months’ trip entirely alone, 
extept for the natives, in tne 
primeval forests and mountains of 
Papua. She has brought a collec- 
tion of 42,400 insects for: the’ 
Natural History Museum ~South 
Kensington, and she expects that 
at least two-thirds of the speci- 
mens she has collected _ be en- 
tirely new. : 

“Most of my work: ud Miss Chees-. 
man told 9 reporter “ was done at 
an altitude of 9,000 feet up in the 
mountainous interior. It ‘rained 
perpetually and most of the time 
I was up to my knees in mud.” 
She found moths, often measuring | 
ten inches across the wings-and of 
extraordinary beauty; stick insects 
ten inches long with sharp spikes 
which could cause a very painful 
wound, and‘ a little orchid which 
caught files.

vy His.most amusing effort was his

tlon with the American war. debt.

present a perusal of the first two

“Peeky” he was very popular: 
among his colleagues.

“Byery successful actor and ac- 
“tress on the” screen possesses 4 
knowledge of drama that, 1s 
instinctive.” 

“Dramatic instinct, is born In a 
player and can never be acquired.” 

These are the opinions of 
Stephen Roberts, . who directed 
Paraniount’s “One Sunday After- 
noon,” starring Gary Cooper, with 
Frances Fuller, Fay Wray and Neil 
Hamilton featured, now showing 
‘at the King’s Theatre. : 

“The dramatic instinct must be 
inherent in' a player who suc- 
ceeds," "says ‘Roberts. “However, 
this crude instinct must be pro- 
perly trained in .the hard schonl 
of experience to obtain’ essential | 
“, technique and polish. 
'  “Fronces” Fuller, who plays 2 
leading «feminine role in “One 
Sunday Afternoon,” ts: a splendid 
example of inborn dramatic 
instinct. Years on the stage have 


gistered ‘strikingly on’ the screen in 
her very first performance. 
“Gary Cooper Js a natural born, 
actor. He has but to appear 
. Natural to give an excellent per- 
formance. In “One Sunday After- 
noon,” he reveals the rare ability 
to wring a tear out of the audience 
at almost the time they are con-. 
vulsed ‘with laughter.” 
. Roberts, who hasbeen directing |. 
pictures for almost twenty. years 
has seen many players come and 
go, during his screen experience. 
His first, movie Job was as assistant 
director for ‘William 8. Hart on the 


old: Hart Westerns., Later, after 


‘a career as 2 short subject, direc- 
* tor, he. spent eight years ‘directing 
comedies. for Educational.

thelr own and -stated that al- 
though the College was in {ts tenth 
year, and: hid so far thrived on 
outside siipport, the time had ar. 
rived when they “shauld seriously j' 
think about housing” the College 
in a building of their own and 
in“such manner. save " consider- 
rable expenditure in rent, an item 
}which ‘constituted’ the biggest 
“bite” in their budget.

years in the existence of the ‘Col- 
‘lege was somewhere in the region

rent itself claimed.the Stupendous

‘ Anniversary .

this school had already completed 
‘its nine years’ work. Munsang is 
now on the threshold of its tenth 
year. The year 1934 is an import- 
art year tor the school. Th marks 


a critical juncture in the school’s. 


life. We have now to decide whe- 
ther the work should be carried 
on” or not ‘to carry on—the, fe 
and death question. Successful as 
our school ‘has been from miany 
Points of view, it still has no home, 
and a school without a homie ot 
its own cannot be considered estab- 
Ushed. For. nine solid years. we 
have -suffered | from Poverty, from 
competition, from’ wiicertainties 
and various hardships. and difMf- 
eWitles for the ‘simple and Obvious 


Teason that the school’ has no |, 


building of its own. As ts shown 


.| bv the figures on record in these 


nine years we have expended, ovet. 
and above the school fees pala by 
the. students, an aggregate sum’ ot 
$59,000.00. 90.4%, of. this money,’ 
ar "$53,360.00 went -to the ‘pocket 
ot" house owners as. rental. This 
is most unfavourable. In fact It 
4s impossible. .The school cannot 
0 on for ever like this. Sooner 
or later we shail, have to face the 
housing problem and we rather 
choose to face it. now, for ‘the 
miore ‘we ‘linger the: more we. will 
lose. So::far * ‘Munsang ° has. never 


appealed: to. the public for finan- } 


clal support) the money which we

by the Government. and partly by 
the few friends who take ° keen 
ataaay in our work. “But our

limited, and so in thls great un- 
dertaking of building up a new

| THE ATTITUDE: OF JAPAN 


” Shanghal has suffered recently 
-/-¢ram:two incidents in which British 
subjects: have been manhandled by 
intobs of Japanese hooligans. These, 
idIneidents fn Shanghai are to be 
| attributed directly to that ‘un- 
{fortunate day when. Japanese 

.croops making use of the Honkew 
settlement as a base, attacked the 


Chinese in Chapel The whole, 


action of the Japanese was in- 
excusable, as the Lytton report 
‘clearly declared: The destruction 
of Chapel, with all the loss. of 
| private property, and the ‘killing of 


women and childzen by Japanese. 
4 


| aeroplanes, is'"a terrible con- 
demnation of the state of Japanese 
civillsation,. An. army by force of 
lige to ws smay be led to: cause 
‘gath to women and . children 
chcidentally. The deliberate action 


os the Japanese armed forces. was 
Vabborrent and repugnant to ‘all 


' adcent minded - people. “That the 
awe ‘take “action 
a 


w 


to Mr.’ 


(783° 


| Shanghal 


Manufacturers, 


i 


iy et : ” 
-jagains, China after their own 


fashion’ is no concern of Great 
Britain; but that such action 
‘should. be taken’ from a settlement 
founded by. and protected “by 
British police’ cannot for one 
minute be excused.. What"is the 


'result ta-day? If one Js.to belleve 


the reports from Shanghai, the 


| police cannot do their’ duty in 


Honkew without molestation from 
Japanesé. armed forces. Why do 
not these armed forces control the 
mobs of Japanese Jnooligans? Ob- 
viously beeause’ they do not want 
to. Ibis high time that steps were 
taken. to ‘inform the Japanese 


government that Britain does not’ 


intend to accept any more of such 
Ineidents. If the. Japanese forces 


who, without any authority, have’ 


usurped the functions of the 
police, cannot keep order, but 
become ever a source of disorder. 


fy Britain afraid of? Any one ‘who 
has seen the Japanese ‘armed 
forces knows that they are no 
match for first 


firm action on'the part, of Britain 
will have any but satisfactory. 
fesults, The Japanese ‘people 
themselves are not at all imbued 
with this fantastic ‘military spirit 


‘hat has arisen. They are peace- |" 


ful and.loveable. If the Japanese’ 
ilitarists are not to be checked. 
then the’ British authorities in 
are taking the big 
responsibility of encouraging them. 
Because that is what happens. 
The Japanese militarists become 
more, and more objectionable and 
their Insolence increases. They- 
think that anything is permissible 
and that the excuses of a consular 
official are ‘sufficient atonement. 


Part of the indecision which 
seems to relgn may be due to the 
development. of communications. 
tn the past British officials were 
compelled to act upon their own 
inittative.- In these days of rapid 
telegraphs .perhaps, the home gov- 
crnment does not. realise the 
situation as {t ts, Hence the 
officials out in the East do not 
have the free hand which. is essen- 
jal to rapid decisions. Whatever 
may be -the cat ses,which lead to 
a policy of drifting, in, this case 
of. the Shanghai ‘Settlement and 
the maintenance of order therein, 
ft is sincerely-to ‘be hoped that 
éteps. Will be taken to ensure that 
the prestige of the flag will in no

x MEET TING 


At the fortnightly meeting of 
the Sanitary Board yesterday, 


Mr,R. A. D. Forrest, the President,” 


announced that in regard to: the 
fees for, special food licenses in 
the districts of Tai Hang, North 
Point and Shaukiwan, a new set 
of ordinance to replace the Public 


Health ‘and Building Ordinance. 


was at present under considera- 


tion, but it -was diffitult to say’ 


when" it would be ready. Yn ‘any 
case the Government..had decreed 
that this aiteration of fees, should 
walt in order to avoid unnecessary 
legislation until the new orders 
were decided’. | 4

STAFF COLLEGE CHANGES 


Captain R, H..T. Raikes, DB.0., 
is to relinquish to-day the post of 
Director of, the R.N. Staff College, 
Greenwich,"on the completion of 
his tenure in command. He ‘is. to 
tecome Commodore and Chief of 
Staff in the Mediterranean Fleet. 
to Admiral Sir William Fisher, in 
sugcession to Rear-Admiral A, E.” 
FP. Bedford, C.B. who will go to 
Bombay to command the Royal 
Indian Marine. Captain G, J. A. 
Miles, Deputy Director, will act as 
Director of the Staff College until 
the arrival of Captain B. C, Wat- 
son, D8.0,, now in command of 
the battleship Valiant. 


Captain ‘Watson has, served on 
the staff of the War- College, 
| Greefiwich,-and the" Tactical 
School, Portsmouth. He went to se: 
from thé Britannia 31 years ago as, 
midshipman ofthe Majestic when 
she was flagship of the late Ad- 
miral Lord Beresford. During. the’ 
War he was navigator of “Admiral 
Tyrwhitt’s flagships at Harwich, 
the Arethusa, Centaur, and Cura- 
|coa, .He has twice: served’ in. the 
Plans Divisidn at the Admiralty, 
and has commanded the sloop 
Hollyhock in’China and the cruiser 
Curlew in the Mediterranean. -'-
